journey to reconsider Brexit. He intends to set out options that could
include rejoining the customs union or single market or possibly the
EU itself, although this is controversial even within his own party and
would in any case only happen in the next Parliament.
UNITED STATES
Resilient activity, struggling housing market and more Fed hikes

ahead. October headline activity data were soft, but the details held
up better. Industrial production fell 0.4% m/m, largely because of a
4.3% drop in utilities, while manufacturing output rose 0.3% m/m and
its annual growth reached 2.1%, the fastest since 2022. Retail sales
slowed to 0.2% m/m, but the GDP-relevant control group accelerated
to 0.6%. Housing remains the weak spot: starts rebounded 9.9% after
three months of sharp declines, but permits fell 7.5% and the NAHB

homebuilder index dropped to its lowest since 2012. Minutes from the
27-28 October FOMC meeting showed that “almost all participants”
expected further tightening under their baseline outlook and FOMC
members comments this week confirmed these views, albeit with disa-
greement over the pace and scale of extra tightening needed. We have
added one rate hike in 2027 to our earlier forecast, to take the Fed Fund
rate to 4.5-4.75% by mid-year.
Upcoming: Q3 GDP second estimate, Fed Beige Book, October PCE and

core PCE inflation, October durable-goods orders and new-home sales
(Wednesday).
JAPAN
Inflation moves further above target. Headline CPI rose 0.8% m/m and
2.3% y/y in October as energy prices increased for the first time since
July. Underlying pressures strengthened as the new core measure ex -
cluding fresh food and energy accelerated to 0.5% m/m. BoJ Governor
Kazuo Ueda expressed confidence that consumption would rebound
after disappointing Q3 GDP and reiterated that raising the policy rate

remained warranted.
TO WATCH NEXT WEEK
Major economies: Flash PMIs for November (Monday).
EMERGING ECONOMIES
ASIA
The AI-led equity market sell-off is spreading.
- Hong Kong Hang Seng Index: -5.2% between 13 November and 20 No-
vember;
- China CSI 300 Index: -1.5% between 13 November and 20 November
(of which IT subindex: -5.5%);
- South Korea Kospi Index: -8% between 13 November and 20 No -
vember and -30.1% since 1 October;
- Taiwan TWSE: -17.5% between 13 November and 20 November and

-25.5% since 1 October. The KRW and TWD have held up.
Southeast Asia is still one of the world’s strongest growth spots. Over
the first nine months of 2026, Vietnam led the region with 9% growth,
followed by Singapore at 6% and Malaysia at 5.6%, while Thailand and
the Philippines lagged with GDP growing at 2.8% and 2.5%. The diver -
gence largely reflects the stronger boost from AI-related investment
and semiconductor and electronics exports in the more technolo -

gy-oriented economies.
LATIN AMERICA
Brazil
Slower activity and fiscal doubts put the real under renewed pressure.
The central bank’s activity index contracted 0.9% q/q in Q3 amid tight
monetary conditions. The Senate approved a fiscal package expected to
improve the primary balance by 0.2pp of GDP, but concerns about the
public-debt trajectory persist. The real has depreciated 9% against the
dollar since President Lula’s re-election, prompting the central bank to

intervene in the foreign-exchange market on 19 November.
EMERGING EUROPE
Central Europe
Hungary : Policy rate on hold. As expected, the Central bank main -
tained its policy rate on hold at 5.5% for the 3 rd consecutive month.
Whilst inflation is still contained (2% y/y in October), it has been rising
and should pick up further in the coming months due to the end of fuel
price caps and the possible end of food margin caps. Meanwhile, the
future shift in the Central bank’s inflation target to 2.5% ±1% in 2028

and the need to anchor inflation expectations suggest some caution
ahead. In the meantime, Hungary’s 10-year sovereign bond yield has
fallen markedly since last April’s elections (-143 bp). In Poland, cur -
rency and bond markets may be more prone to volatility due to increa-
sing political tensions between the Central bank and the Constitutional
court.
Türkiye
Inflation fears and investment fund scandal shake sentiment. On No -
vember 12, the Turkish central bank raised its end-2027 inflation fo -

recast to 21% from 15%, citing the Iran war. Foreign currency deposits
held by Turkish residents rose $5.2 billion in the week through No -
vember 13 — the biggest weekly increase since July.
COMMODITIES
Oil and gas. Prices fall as markets look through geopolitical risks. Des-
pite a flare-up in attacks in the Strait of Hormuz and Saudi Arabia and
renewed rumours of imminent US-Israeli massive strikes on Iran, Brent
fell 0.5% over the week to $92/bbl as markets focused on the expected

year-end crude surplus forecast by the IEA, and improving refined-pro-
duct inventories. US commercial crude inventories increased another
3.4mb to 418mb, while SPR stocks fell 3.2mb. Diesel cracks declined
10% in the US and 8.5% in Europe. European gas prices rose by 11% to
€94/MWh amid forecasts of cold temperatures for the next two weeks.
y/y = year-on-year
m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the
likely pace of economic activity relative to trend three to
nine months into the future, declined to –0.14% in October
from +0.06% in September.

The Leading Index weakened again in October after steadier
results through August-September. At –0.14% the latest six
month growth rate is back in negative, below-trend territory,
broadly similar to the soft reads around the start of the year.
The shift points to a return to below-trend growth momentum
in the first half of 2027. Westpac expects annual GDP growth
to slow back to 1.4% for calendar 2026, with a further
moderation to a sluggish 1%yr pace in 2027.

The Leading Index growth rate is still a touch firmer than
six months ago, the current pace of –0.14% comparing to a
–0.2% back in April, when the War in Iran was first impacting.
The component detail shows mixed moves over the
intervening period. Four components have started to exert
a bigger drag: total hours worked taking –0.2ppts off the
headline growth rate; with a further 0.15ppt drag coming
from the combined effect of softer commodity prices

(measured in AUD terms); a narrowing yield spread (mainly
reflecting the RBA tightening pushing up short term rates);
and rising job loss fears captured by the Westpac-Melbourne
Institute Unemployment Expectations Index. This has been
more than offset by a reduced (but still negative) drag from
the Westpac-Melbourne Institute Consumer Expectations
Index (adding +0.25ppts); and firmer reads on local equity
markets and dwelling approvals (adding +0.17ppts on a

combined basis.
Overall, the component mix shows labour market conditions
and tightening monetary policy have become more prominent
drags on momentum. While the Leading Index signal is not
overly weak the decline is clearly worth monitoring with the
detail suggesting there may be more weakening to come.
Leading Index moves back below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines

variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken
individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

## Document 386: Q3 Growth in advanced economies_ solid despite shocks (2026-10-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Q3 Growth in advanced economies_ solid despite shocks (2026-10-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-23
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 23 October 2026

EcoFlash

Q3 Growth in advanced economies: solid despite shocks

Growth looks to have held up in Q3 despite successive shocks (see our analysis), as it did in 2025 and H1 2026. A longer 
Middle East conflict has fed into more persistent inflation; drought has worsened in many countries, and interest rates 
have risen further – at the short end, with both the ECB and the Fed raising policy rates in September, and even more at 
the long end. However, our nowcasts show that growth is bending without breaking in most advanced economies. GDP 
figures (due on 29 October in the US and 30 October for the euro area and its member states) are expected to confirm 
that. In the Euro area, momentum looks stable (excluding Ireland, whose GDP is volatile). In Germany, growth has been 
uninterrupted since Q4 2025, while France is likely to have returned to growth after stagnating in H1. In the US, growth 
is likely to have gathered pace, according to the GDP Now, still driven by AI, but increasingly by other sectors as well.

Euro area: resilience beneath the surface. Our nowcast points to growth slowing to 0.3% in q/q, though the pace is stable 
excluding Ireland (Irish GDP is volatile and had markedly boosted the Q2 figure). Excluding Ireland, growth should hold at its H2 
average, a sign of the bloc’s underlying resilience. Also, momentum has broadened to a growing number of sectors (see our 
analysis). While industrial production stalled over the summer, services remained buoyant. The services PMI rebounded sharply 
in Q3 and sits firmly in expansion territory (53.0 in September). Improving job-related survey data (PMI and European Commission 
surveys) and a stable unemployment rate (6.4% in August) hint at more hiring in Q3 – a support for household consumption that 
partly offsets purchasing-power losses from persistent inflation. Corporate investment is underpinned by buoyant new investment 
lending (8.7% y/y In August, 12-month cumulative). 
France: growing, nevertheless. The succession of shocks does not appear to have prevented France from returning to 
growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q, according to our nowcast. One-off factors accounted for the stall in activity in H1. In Q3, the drought 
worsened and industrial production remained subdued. However, market services production rebounded in July and August, 
notably in business services, restaurants, digital and real estate. The latter points to residential investment recovering after a weak 
H1. Corporate investment likely followed, as reflected in buoyant credit flows and AI-related investment. Household consumption 
is likely to have improved slightly on higher electric vehicle sales.  
Euro area  France

Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas  Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas

Germany: growth still solid. The H1 rebound in activity is moderating but continues. Our nowcast points to a fourth straight 
quarter of growth in Q3, at 0.2% q/q (after 0.3% in Q4 2025, 0.4% in Q1 2026, and 0.3% in Q2). Manufacturing activity eased 
slightly, according to the September PMI survey (53.9, down 0.4 m/m). Government spending will remain growth-supportive, with 
defence and infrastructure outlays gaining traction. Infrastructure spending continues to underpin activity in public works, which 
stands at its highest since November 2021 (2.5% 3m/3m in August). Exports (4.4% y/y between January and August) remain the 
main growth engine, helped by a pivot towards Europe (7.2%). 
United States: an expansion undeterred by inflation. Growth is likely to have gathered pace in Q3. The Atlanta Fed’s GDP 
Now points to a 3.5% annualized growth rate (AR), close to our own 3.7% forecast (0.9% non-AR) and up from 2.2% in 
Q2. Domestic demand remains the engine. Consumption is weathering the pickup in inflation, while the AI-driven capex cycle 
remains robust – as reflected in retail sales and nondefence capital-goods orders (excl. aircraft). As a result, growth in final sales

### Page 2

to private domestic purchasers1 should print above 4% AR again. By contrast, external trade should remain a drag on growth, 
reflecting AI-related import needs. Survey data point to momentum that is both solid and broad-based. The ISM indices have 
remained firmly in growth territory (54.9 quarterly average for manufacturing and services alike). At the same time, they highlight 
persistent price and supply-chain pressures.

Germany   United States

Source: Refinitiv, BNP Paribas   Source: Refinitiv, Atlanta Fed, BNP Paribas

## Document 401: The Commodities Feed_ Oil extends losses while copper gives back Nvidia bounce

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Commodities Feed_ Oil extends losses while copper gives back Nvidia bounce.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Commodity markets came under renewed pressure on
Tuesday. Copper reversed Monday’s Nvidia-driven rebound
despite continued physical tightness, while oil extended its
decline on hopes of US-Iran talks, and US natural gas fell
sharply on milder weather forecasts

Ewa Manthey and Warren Patterson
Macro concerns are likely to keep copper volatile in the near term
Energy – Oil falls as hopes of US-Iran talks
grow
Oil prices extended their decline on Tuesday, with WTI remaining
below $80/bbl as expectations of potential US-Iran negotiations
continued to unwind some of the geopolitical risk premium. Prices
fell almost 4% on Monday, with WTI touching its lowest level since
August after reports that the US was willing to engage in talks with
Iran.
The prospect of negotiations has eased some immediate concerns
over Middle East supply, particularly as crude exports from the

Persian Gulf have remained relatively resilient despite continued

around $3.1/MMBtu, the biggest daily decline since February. Milder
weather forecasts across key consuming regions have weighed on
heating demand expectations, while US production remains close
to record levels. The combination of softer weather-driven demand
and strong supply should continue to weigh on prices in the near

term.
Metals – Copper gives back Nvidia bounce
Copper gave back Monday’s Nvidia-driven gains on Tuesday, falling
more than 2% towards $12,000/t as focus shifted back to Chinese
demand and US interest rates.
The latest weakness is largely a ﬁnancial market story rather than
a broad deterioration in copper fundamentals. The Chinese
physical market remains tight, with elevated import premiums and
nearby spreads signalling limited availability. Some of the copper

accumulated in the US earlier this year is beginning to return to the
international market, but this should take time to materially ease
tightness elsewhere.
Aluminium is experiencing similar near-term constraints, reﬂected
in a sharp widening of the LME cash-to-three-month spread above
$400/t on Monday as exchange availability is at historical lows.
More Indonesian supply should eventually help, although much of
the new material is not yet deliverable against the LME.

## Document 426: US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials

Source PDF: all report pdf/US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-23
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials
Date:    1 hour ago
US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US SECTORS CALL: Information
Technology, Communication
Services & Financials
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 22 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Steve A Johnson / Unsplash

### Page 2

Last week was brutal. All 11 S&P 500 sectors fell sharply. The S&P 500
dropped 3.7% on Friday alone. The index has fallen for seven days in a
row, its longest losing streak since February 2020. It is up just 1.0% ytd.
The Nasdaq is on its longest weekly losing streak since 2008. The S&P
500 Semiconductors index is down 28.1% mtd.
Last week, we compared November's Friday the 13th S&P 500 close to
August 3, when the index first rose to the same level. We noted that while

the index was relatively calm over that period, there was lots of turbulence
below the surface in the S&P 500 sectors. The calm broke last week. The
S&P 500 is down 9.1% since August 3, and only three sectors remain
above water. Health Care (OW) and Consumer Staples (UW) are up 9.2%
and 8.6% (chart). Energy (OW) is up 2.8% and remains the best sector
performer ytd at a 34.8% gain. Communication Services (MW) is down
19.7%, with Information Technology (MW) and Industrials (OW) down

15.2% and 14.7%.

### Page 3

So far, the selloff has been driven more by increasingly bearish valuation
multiples than by analysts' consensus earnings estimates, which remain
bullish. Let's have a closer look at Information Technology,
Communication Services, and Financials:
(1) Information Technology. Investors don't believe the analysts'
earnings forecasts. Analysts expect IT earnings to grow 58.4% this year
and 48.4% next year. They expect Semiconductors earnings to grow
114.6% and 77.8% this year and next and Semiconductor Equipment

earnings to grow 36.3% and 51.3%. Investors are not paying for that. The
forward P/E of the sector is down 41.9% ytd, while forward earnings is up
71.0% (chart). The stock price index is down 0.7%, after rising over 30%
in early October.

### Page 4

Semiconductors show the gap most clearly. The industry accounts for
54.9% of IT's forward earnings and 38.0% of its market cap. Its forward
P/E has fallen to 9.4, well below the S&P 500's 16.6, and is near its 2010-
11 lows (chart).
Semiconductor Equipment is no different. Its forward earnings is up 89.7%
ytd, while its forward P/E is down 41.8% (chart).
Application Software is the exception. Analysts cut its 2027 earnings
growth forecast to 9.5% from about 17% in August (chart). It is down

28.6% ytd, the worst in the Information Technology industry.

### Page 5

We lowered our sector recommendation to market-weight late last year.
We are sticking with it because we believe the AI buildout will survive CEO
Jensen Huang's resignation from Nvidia.
(2) Communication Services. Meta and Alphabet make up about 40% of
the sector. Both have been caught up in the AI selloff. Meta is down 28%
from its ytd peak, and Alphabet is down 34%. The sector is down 15.5%
ytd, the worst of all 11 S&P 500 sectors. Forward earnings is up 21.5%

ytd, while the forward P/E is down 26.0% (chart).
Analysts expect Interactive Media & Services revenues to grow 25.8% in
2027. Yet they expect earnings to fall 14.2% (chart). Much of that reflects
a base effect from this year's mark-to-market gains posted by Alphabet.

### Page 6

The forward profit margin has also dropped to 26.4% from almost 32%
this summer, partly reflecting the reversal of the mark-to-market impact
next year.
We retain our market-weight rating on Communication Services because a
slower pace of the AI buildout should boost the profitability and cash flow
of the Magnificent-7 including Alphabet and Meta.
(3) Financials. Investors are treating parts of the sector as an AI trade,
which has dragged the whole group down. Investment Banking &

Brokerage fell 11.7% this week, trading down in step with IT. Last week,
we noted how the sector's forward P/E had fallen to 15.2. It is now 14.2,
down from 16.4 at the end of 2025 (chart). Forward earnings is still up
14.1% ytd.

### Page 7

Insurance has been the shelter from the storm. The Property & Casualty
Insurance price index is near a record high, and Insurance Brokers trades
11.8% above its 200-day moving average. Property & Casualty
Insurance's forward profit margin of 13.7% is the highest since 2007
(chart). Analysts expect earnings to grow 11.9% this year but to decline
8.4% next year, so the margin is likely peaking.
Consumer Finance is especially at risk. The industry’s price index is down

21.6% ytd, with a forward P/E that has dropped to 10.2. Yet analysts
expect that industry’s earnings to grow 11.3% this year and 11.4% next
year (chart). The industry is getting cheaper even as earnings grow.
Investors worry that Washington will cap credit card interest rates next
year.

### Page 8

Analysts expect Financials' earnings growth to slow to 7.4% next year
from 16.9% this year. Some de-rating makes sense. But with the sector
trading at 14.2 times forward earnings, we think the market has already
priced in the slowdown. We retain our overweight rating on Financials.

## Document 434: What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-10-11
Pages: 17
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it?
A closer look at the impact of China’s currency on China’s export growth.
Sean Yong/Reuters
UPDATED
October 11, 2026 12:00 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
There is an argument—made in the recent Geneva
Report and by various former IMF officials—that
China’s current export surge is the simple
consequence of the housing downturn and the
resulting weakness in Chinese internal demand.*
The weakness in the RMB in this story is entirely
incidental to the increase in exports, which is, more or
less, a mechanical response to weak internal demand.

Cannot sell excavators at home? Export them.
Built more modern car capacity than your internal
market supports? Export that too…
The fall in the nominal RMB over the course of 2022
and the first half of 2023 in nominal and read terms
was simply a function of domestic macroeconomic
weakness, and a growing rate differential. It wasn’t an
active policy choice by China’s central bank.

### Page 3

3
Now, some might note that China’s currency doesn’t
always move with rate differentials. The RMB didn’t
consistently get pulled up when Chinese rates were
higher than U.S. rates (2002-2020 generally
speaking), and while it did fall when rate differentials
moved against the RMB in 2022 and 2023, it then
stabilized and has even appreciated modestly since
the middle of last year. That appreciation hasn’t been
big enough to correct the deep undervaluation of the

RMB, but it came at a time when the PBOC policy
rate (the reverse repo rate) was well below the U.S.
policy rate.
China doesn’t have an open financial account. As a
result, equilibrium in the foreign exchange market is
thus driven far more by exporter conversion and
current account flows than by rate differentials and
financial flows.** That, together with China’s ongoing
management of the exchange rate and a trade surplus
that can generate significant inflows, means that the

### Page 4

4
value of the RMB is much more of a policy variable
under the independent control of the Chinese
authorities than a simple byproduct of monetary and
fiscal policies.
The modern IMF is reluctant to accept this
conclusion. Its surveillance has generally ignored
China’s currency management (even if that
management is relatively obvious; see the data on FX
settlement) and its recent comprehensive assessment
of China’s economy completely ignored the balance of

payments and exchange rate management.
But it is pretty obvious to the market, even if the
PBOC, rather absurdly, insists that “market forces
play a decisive role in determining the RMB
exchange rate.”
I doubt the PBOC can win this argument. I agree
with Mark Sobel:
“The renminbi is still heavily managed and the
authorities have acted to slow appreciation. The paper
states that the PBoC phased out ‘regular’ foreign
exchange intervention in 2017. But it has many

means at its disposal to achieve the same effect
through extensive capital controls, administrative
guidance, signalling at the fix and deploying state-
owned commercial banks. These means are heavily
used. Thus, the word ‘regular’ must be taken with a
pinch of salt. The comparison to Japan, which

### Page 5

5
intervenes occasionally, is spurious – the yen largely
floats freely in integrated global markets. The same
cannot be said for the renminbi.”
In reality, the PBOC’s daily fix now plays the decisive
role in the currency’s path, and the market is often a
bet on how the PBOC’s management of the yuan will
evolve.
That is clear from the lack of volatility in the yuan’s
rate against the dollar. The yuan’s move down against
the dollar in 2022 was a controlled 10 percent move,

and the appreciation over the last 16 months has been
even more controlled crawl.
And the market is clearly massaged with substantial
help from the state banks. In fact that the pattern of
changes in the monthly FX settlement data looks
exactly like the pattern of intervention of a central
bank managing its currency around a tight band (the

### Page 6

6
state banks in practice buy FX to keep the CNY from
testing the strong side of the band, so there is an
asymmetry in how the CNY is managed)
None of this would matter much if China’s economy
was small. But that obviously isn’t the case. Nor would
this matter much if China’s exports didn’t respond to
changes in the RMB’s real (and nominal) value. But
that is also not the case.
There is abundant evidence that China’s exports do
respond to moves in the currency. Periods of RMB

weakness have consistently been associated with
periods of especially rapid Chinese export growth.

### Page 7

7
Chancellor Merz and President Macron got this
right, even if macroeconomic issues haven’t yet
figured prominently in the EU’s dialogue with
China.*** As they note in their open letter to the
European Commission:
“An appreciation of undervalued currencies towards a value
more in line with fundamentals would help support the
rebalancing of trade accounts with the EU in the short and
medium term … European policy discussions focused on
forging a common understanding of these exchange rate issues

and their interaction with global imbalances would be useful.”
The best evidence that exports responded to changes
in the currency, and not simply to changes in the path
of China’s internal demand, is the acceleration in
China’s export growth after the RMB’s depreciation
over the course of 2022.
This is the killer chart.

### Page 8

8
Now, you might ask: what of the acceleration in
export growth in 2020 and 2021? That was pretty
clearly tied to the global increase in goods demand
that came with the pandemic and work from home
requirements. It was expected to fade when the world
economy normalized. But it didn’t. By 2023 Chinese
export growth again topped global trade growth.
The general expectation that the rise in China’s goods
surplus during Covid would prove to be “transitory”

### Page 9

9
heavily influenced the IMF’s work at the time: see the
IMF’s external sector reports of 2023 and 2024.
I would argue that neglecting the exchange rate was
the key reason the IMF’s kept forecasting, incorrectly,
that China’s current account surplus would fall (See
this blog with Shahin Vallee). The IMF certainly
didn’t warn that China’s property downturn and the
RMB’s real depreciation risked a second China shock
in its 2022, 2023, or 2024 surveillance reports.

Concretely, the weaker RMB appears to have
contributed to a very large outperformance of China’s
exports relative to global trade. See the following
chart
That incidentally also happened after the RMB
followed the dollar down in 2003.
In both periods, there were significant structural
shifts. The WTO accession in 2002, and the success

### Page 10

10
of Chinese industrial policies in the “green” industrial
sectors and autos in 2022. In both cses exports were
turbocharged when structural shifts that should have
resulted in a stronger currency were instead amplified
by currency weakness.
No matter. Paul Krugman has noted that a country
that is converging technologically with its trade
partners should see a trend appreciation in its
currency. China got the technological convergence,
but not the real appreciation—at least not until this

year. And the move since the middle of 2025 has
corrected the depreciation of the RMB that occurred
when the RMB followed the dollar down on
liberation day, but not the structural overvaluation.
Now, there is an argument that exchange rate
adjustment is too hot an issue to make it a priority.
The PBOC isn’t interested it seems.
And some believe that in the interest of comity and
good relations with China, the IMF should mirror
the PBOC’s views. After all a stronger RMB would

slow China’s exports and thus slow China’s economy
at a time when China needs export growth to escape
deflation and generate employment. Or so the
argument goes. Concerns about “external balance“
and the negative global spillovers from China’s rising
goods surplus should be ignored and the Fund’s sole
focus should be on policies that lead to internal

### Page 11

11
balance. That provides one rationale for the Fund’s
ongoing decision to essentially ignore the contentious
issue of the PBOC’s ongoing exchange rate
management.
That argument, however, assumes that China’s
economy is small and thus China’s external surplus
can safely be ignored because it isn’t a drag on the
growth of the IMF’s other members.
That clearly isn’t true.
And it isn’t clear that China is more interested in
having a negotiation over its level of basic pension

benefits or the size of its fiscal deficit (where the
IMF continues to make timid recommendations that
would not move the needle—and certainly wouldn’t
meaningfully reduce China’s external surplus) than a
discussion over its currency practices.****
Back in the world of fixed but adjustable exchange
rates, there was an expectation (not always met to be
sure) that surplus countries would allow their
currencies to strengthen to bring their economies

back into external balance, and that other policy tools
would be used to ensure internal balance.
I would argue that China’s exchange rate (against
both the dollar and the basket) has many similarities
to the old world of fixed but adjustable currencies.
True, it moves on a controlled path when it moves

### Page 12

12
between different levels rather than jumping up and
down. But the PBOC does frequently target a level,
and that targeted level does change over time.
I would further argue that steps like the recent tariff
rate quota that will limit China’s exports of hybrid
cars to Europe to around 350,000 units next year (up
from 2025, but well below the 800,000 units of
2026) aren’t going to address the underlying
imbalance on their own. They can provide a bit of a

buffer for specific sectors.
But that buffer, while necessary, won’t address the
broad-based erosion of manufacturing capacity
outside China. This erosion will persist as long as an
undervalued RMB sends the entire world a signal to
use China as its production base.
The new system of managed trade that is emerging in
autos would need to be progressively expanded to an
ever-increasing set of sectors if protection is used as a
substitute for both domestic reform in China and

exchange rate adjustment.
That is now the direction of travel, but it doesn’t
address the underlying systemic issues.
Incidentally, those who believe the path of internal
demand is critical to rebalancing and that exchange
rate moves are secondary need to explain how an
acceleration in domestic demand would, on its own,

### Page 13

13
lead China’s external accounts to adjust. One
important, stylized fact about China is that domestic
demand growth is no longer feeding over to import
growth (ex-gold, that is).
Bottom line: China, which needs a 1.5 percentage
point contribution from net exports every year, is on a
collision course with its trading partners without a
currency correction that provides the basis for more
balanced trade even in the absence of trade
restrictions.
And there isn’t a realistic path towards balance that

doesn’t involve a stronger RMB; there aren’t, as Paul
Krugman once put it, immaculate transfers.
* That downturn shows up in domestic deflationary
pressure, and weak job growth/high rates of (at least
youth) unemployment. China, in other words, isn’t in
“internal balance.” I really liked the report from

### Page 14

14

arguing China needs two tools to hit two targets
(internal and external balance).
** To be sure, rate differentials matter for exporter
conversion—but so does the expected movement of
the RMB. The market understands this better than

Sachs) note uses a framework similar to mine to
describe the CNY’s moves in Q3:
“In our view, sizeable FX inflows have continued to support

RMB appreciation despite unfavorable interest rate
differentials, as strong export receipts and increased exporter
FX conversions have outweighed portfolio outflows. Our
estimated FX conversion ratio rose to 63% in August,
compared with around 45% in normal years, likely reflecting a
shift in market expectations from RMB depreciation to
appreciation.”
*** Š ef č ovi č  has jurisdiction over trade policy
(narrowly defined), but not broader macroeconomic

policies. Europe’s institutional architecture for
exchange rate coordination is not well-developed.
**** The current account surplus is no longer masked
to the same extent as in 2023 and 2024, as SAFE has
fiddled with its BoP methodology once again (per the
Federal Reserve) and the BoP goods surplus is no
longer significantly below the customs goods surplus.
Of course, there are still problems in the data—the
deficit in the investment income account most

## Document 438: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising”. He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

### Page 4

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

### Page 5

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Figure 2: Gold price (USD/oz, log scale)
10
100
1000
1920 1940 1960 1980 2000 2020
In the Bretton Woods
era, the dollar was tied
to gold
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

### Page 7

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
Figure 3: DM economies have avoided systemic financial
crises since the post-GFC period
Source : Laeven and Valencia (2026).

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)
35%
40%
45%
50%
2002 2006 2010 2014 2018 2022
Banks Nonbank Financial Intermediation Sector (NBFI)
Source :Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

### Page 12

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 12 Deutsche Bank AG
the role of credit events more in the next section.

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.

Conclusion
Whilst a major financial crisis has been avoided since the GFC and the subsequent
sovereign crisis, the problem of financial instability has yet to be permanently

solved. On the one hand, reforms to the financial system and the swift actions of
policymakers have helped to prevent a repeat. But as we know from Hyman
Minsky’s warning, lengthy periods of stability can often lay the foundations for the
next crisis, even if these take place slowly. Indeed, risks often shift rather than
disappear.
Looking forward, there are several vectors of risk worth paying attention to. But
financial crises often start in areas where people are paying little attention, or risks

are assumed to be contained. Indeed, it is the areas where risk is underappreciated
which often prove the most vulnerable.
As such, predicting where the next crisis might arise is inherently difficult. But the
topic is a vital one, given the long-lasting damage that financial crises can cause
when they do occur. Indeed, with the fiscal space for sovereigns increasingly
constrained today, the margin for a future policy error is limited.

○ ブラジル中央銀行は、11 月３～４日に開催した定例会合において、政策金利を 13.75％に据

ていることを警戒し、物価安定を優先する慎重姿勢を強めている。先行きの金融政策につい
ては、ルラ政権が財政健全化に向けた具体策を示し、市場の信認を回復できるかが重要とな
る。財政不安が後退し、レアル相場やインフレ期待が安定すれば利下げ再開の余地は広がる
ものの、財政拡張への懸念が払拭されなければ高金利政策が長期化する可能性がある。 
○ 高金利の長期化は景気減速や政府の利払い負担の増大を通じて財政悪化を招くことが懸念さ
れるため、財政政策と金融政策の整合性が一段と重要になる。当面のレアル相場は、財政運
営に対する市場の評価に左右される不安定な展開が続くと見込まれる。

ているとの認識が示された。海外経済については、イラン情勢の長期化に伴う原油価格や石油製品

減速が従来想定よりも鮮明になり、労働
市場にも頭打ちの兆しがみられるとし

加速しており、中銀が掲げる目標レンジ
（３±1.5％）の上限を上回っているこ
とに警戒を示した（図２）。 
金融市場において、先行きのインフレ期待が一段と上昇していることにも注意が必要である。中
銀が金融機関などを対象に実施する市場予想調査（Focus）では、2026 年のインフレ率見通しは９
月会合時点の＋4.9％から＋5.2％へ、2027 年も＋4.3％から＋4.8％に上方修正されている。さら
に、中銀自身が示した金融政策の効果を評価する対象期間である2028 年４-６月時点のインフレ率
見通しも＋3.5％と、目標を上回る水準にとどまっている。このため、インフレ率が上振れするリス

3/5 
クが下振れリスクを上回っているとしたうえで、通貨安の長期化による輸入物価への波及や、原油
価格上昇の二次的な影響、財政刺激策による需要の押し上げなどを警戒する姿勢を示した。 
先行きの政策運営については、具体的な利下げ再開時期を示すことはせず、物価目標への収束に
必要な金融引き締めの程度を維持する方針を強調した。９月会合まで記されていた金融緩和の継続
を示唆する文言は削除されるなど緩和姿勢は後退しており、金融緩和の継続を前提としない姿勢に
変化している様子がうかがえる。しかし、中銀は現行の金利水準について、十分に引き締め的と評
価しており、景気減速の強まりも認識している。このため、今回の判断は直ちに金融引き締めへの
転換を示すものではなく、インフレ期待や金融市場の動向を見極めるための予防的な利下げ休止と
考えられる。

【金融市場は、ルラ政権の財政運営がインフレ期待や金利の上昇圧力を強める可能性を警戒】 
前述のように、同国金融市場においては、大統領選挙の決選投票の結果を受けて、翌 26 日にブラ
ジル資産を売却する動きが急速に広が
った。その後は、主要株価指数、レアル
相場ともに一定の持ち直しの動きをみ
せたものの、不安定な動きが続いている
（図３）。こうした動きは、金融市場の混
乱が一時的なポジション調整による側
面を有する一方、ブラジルの財政運営に
対する投資家の警戒感が払拭されてい
ないことを示唆している。こうしたな
か、今後の金融市場の動向を左右する重
要な焦点となるのが、ルラ政権の財政運
営である。ルラ政権は、低所得者層への所得再分配や社会保障の拡充、公共投資を重視する政策を
掲げてきた。決選投票の前には、低所得者向け現金給付制度（ボルサ・ファミリア）の給付額を 15％
引き上げる方針などを打ち出した。ルラ政権は、既存予算の組み替えなどによって財政ルールや基
礎的財政収支の目標を維持する方針を示しているものの、社会保障支出や公共投資の拡大と財政健
全化を両立できるかについて、金融市場では慎重な見方が強まっている。

### Page 4

4/5 
ブラジル政府が８月末に提出した2027 年予算案では、基礎的財政収支についてＧＤＰ比0.5％の
黒字目標を掲げ、財政ルールに基づく調整後の黒字額を 834 億レアルと見込んでいる。しかし、調
整前の黒字額は 186 億レアルにとどま
る。一方で、10 月時点の民間予測によれ
ば、2027 年の中央政府の基礎的財政収支
は440 億レアル程度の赤字となる見通し
となっている。さらに、同調査では、一
般政府の債務残高が 2026 年のＧＤＰ比
83.4％から 2027 年には 87.0％へ上昇す
ると見込まれており（図４）、財政の持続
可能性を巡る懸念は根強く残る。こうし
たなか、財政政策に対する信認が十分に
回復しなければ、国債利回りの高止まり
やレアル安を通じて金融環境の不安定化が続くことが予想される。 
とはいえ、ルラ政権が財政規律を放棄する方針を示していると判断するのは早計である。ドゥリ
ガン財務相は、選挙後の金融市場の混乱について、それまで積み上がっていたポジションの巻き戻
しによる影響が大きいとの認識を示すとともに、政府として財政規律を維持する方針をあらためて
強調している。このため、今後の金融市場の動向は、財政支出の規模だけでなく、歳出抑制策や増
収策の具体化、2027 年予算案の実現可能性、経済政策を担う閣僚の人選などによって左右されると
考えられる。 
中銀も、声明のなかで、国内の財政政策が金融政策や金融資産の価格形成に及ぼす影響を引き続
き注視する方針を明示している。財政拡張による国内需要の押し上げに加え、財政不安を背景とす
るレアル安やインフレ期待の上昇が続けば、物価の安定に必要な金利水準が高止まりする可能性が
ある。さらに、高金利の長期化は政府の利払い負担を増大させ、財政状況を一段と悪化させる要因
となり得る。このため、今後のブラジル経済を巡っては、財政政策と金融政策の整合性を確保でき
るかが重要な課題になると考えられる。

【先行きの政策運営は財政政策への信認回復がカギを握る一方、レアル相場には不透明感が残る】 
先行きの金融政策については、今回の利下げ休止を受けて、高金利政策が当初想定よりも長期化
する可能性が高まっている。大統領選挙後に公表された Focus 調査では、2026 年末の政策金利見通
しが従来の 13.25％から 13.50％に、2027 年末についても 11.75％から 12.50％へと引き上げられ
た。これは、金融市場においては、中銀による段階的な利下げ再開を想定しているものの、そのペ
ースは従来に比べて緩やかになると見込んでいることを示している。 
今後の政策運営を巡っては、ルラ政権が財政健全化に向けた具体策を示すことにより、金融市場
の信認を回復できるかが重要となる。財政運営への懸念が後退し、レアル相場が安定、インフレ期
待の低下が確認されれば、中銀が段階的な利下げを再開する余地は広がると考えられる。一方で、

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告

りません。
5/5
財政拡張に対する懸念が払拭されず、レアル安やインフレ期待の上昇が続く場合には、金融緩和の
休止が一段と長期化する可能性がある。さらに、原油価格の高止まりなど外部環境の悪化が重なれ
ば、追加的な金融引き締めを迫られる展開も否定できない。
一方で、足元のブラジル経済は、これまでの高金利政策の影響で内需を中心とする景気減速の動
きが鮮明になりつつある。高金利の長期化は、家計消費や企業の設備投資を抑制するだけでなく、
政府の資金調達コストを押し上げることを通じて財政状況にも影響を与える。このため、財政運営
に対する信認の回復が遅れる場合、中銀は景気下支えに向けた金融緩和と、インフレ抑制に向けた
金融引き締めの間で難しい判断を迫られることになる。

当面のレアル相場は、ルラ政権の財政政策に対する市場の評価に左右されやすい展開が続くと考
えられる。財政健全化に向けた実効性のある政策が示されれば、国債利回りの低下や海外資金の流
入を通じてレアル相場が持ち直す余地はある。しかし、具体的な方向性が示されない場合には、財
政リスクを警戒した資金流出が続く可能性がある。中銀にとっては、財政政策に起因する金融市場
の不安定化が物価安定の妨げとなることをいかに回避するかが、今後の政策運営における重要な課
題になると見込まれる。

米国

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー

2026 年 11 月 11 日 全 10 頁

主要先進国・地域 金融政策モニター 
2026年10 月～11 月：利上げの持続性を左右するのは企業の期待イン
フレ率

経済調査部 エコノミスト 中村 華奈子

[要約] 
 日本を除く主要先進国・地域の中央銀行は 2024 年から 25 年にかけて利下げを進めて
きたが、2026 年に入ると、中東情勢の緊迫化に伴うエネルギー価格の上昇などからイ
ンフレ率が再加速し、利上げに転じる動きが広がっている。2026 年 10 月 1 日～11 月 5
日に開催された金融政策決定会合では、カナダ、オーストラリア、スウェーデン、イ
ギリスの 4 カ国が利上げを決定した。アメリカやユーロ圏、日本など 5 カ国・地域は
政策金利を据え置いたが、いずれも 9 月までに利上げを実施しており、今回の据え置
きは、これまでの利上げの効果を見極めるための様子見とみられる。 
 OIS 市場の織り込みを見ると、日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏・イギリスのいずれも 2027
年にかけて追加利上げが見込まれている。2027 年 9 月にかけての利上げ幅は最大で
0.7％pt 程度（1 回当たり 0.25％pt 刻みとすれば 2～3 回程度）だ。他方、利上げのタ
イミングを見ると、日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏では 2026 年 12 月会合の利上げ確率が 6
割～8 割弱程度と高い。一方、2027 年の各会合では 1 割弱～5 割程度でばらついてお
り、利上げペースについて市場の見方は定まっていない。 
 2027 年以降も利上げが続くか否かは、中長期の期待インフレ率がアンカーされている
かに左右される。アンカーされていれば、中央銀行は供給ショックによる物価上昇を
見過ごせるが、そうでなければ利上げの継続を迫られる。中でも、価格や賃金を実際
に設定する企業の期待インフレ率が重要だ。日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏・イギリスの
企業の期待インフレ率は、2022 年から 23 年のインフレ高進期に上振れした後、2024
年から 25 年にかけて低下した。もっとも、足元の期待インフレ率は、実績のインフレ
率が 2％程度の場合に対応する水準の目安を上回っており、とりわけ日本では上振れ
幅が大きい。中東での紛争に終わりが見えず、原油高といった供給ショックが長引く
中、企業の期待インフレ率が一段と上方シフトする可能性に注意が必要だ。

### Page 2

1

2 / 10

主要先進国・地域 1の中央銀行が 2026 年 10 月 1 日～11 月 5 日に開催した金融政策決定会合
（以下、会合）では、カナダ、オーストラリア、スウェーデン、イギリスの 4 カ国が政策金利を
引き上げ、アメリカやユーロ圏、日本など 5 カ国・地域 2が据え置いた。本レポートでは、各
中央銀行の金融政策動向を定点観測する。今月のトピックでは、2027 年以降の利上げの持続性
を占う材料として、企業の期待インフレ率を取り上げる。

１ 主要先進国・地域の金融政策 
政策金利：利上げに転じる中央銀行が増える一方、各行の動きにはばらつき 
図表 1 は、主要先進国・地域の政策金利の推移を示したものだ。日本を除く 9 カ国・地域の
中央銀行は、2024 年から 2025 年にかけてインフレ率の鈍化を背景に利下げを進めてきたが、
2026 年に入ると、インフレ率の再加速を受けて利上げに転じる中央銀行が相次いでいる。 
直近（2026 年 10 月 1 日～11 月 5 日）の会合を見ると、カナダ、オーストラリア、スウェー
デン、イギリスが利上げを決定した。カナダは 10 月 28 日に政策金利を 2.50％へ引き上げ、
2023 年以来の利上げとなった。オーストラリアは 11 月 3 日に 4.85％へ引き上げ、年初来 5 回
目の利上げとなった。スウェーデンも 11 月 4 日に 2.00％へと引き上げたことを公表した。イ
ギリスは 11 月 5 日に 4.00％へ引き上げ、2023 年 8 月以来の利上げとなった。他方、アメリカ
（10 月 28 日）、ニュージーランド（同日）、ユーロ圏（10 月 29 日）、日本（10 月 30 日）、ノル
ウェー（11 月 5 日、公表日）は政策金利を据え置いた。もっとも、これらの中央銀行はいずれ
も 9 月までに利上げを実施しており、今回の据え置きは、これまでの利上げの効果を見極める
ための様子見とみられる。なお、スイスは同期間に会合がなく、政策金利は 0.00％のままであ
る。 
今次利上げ局面で、他の中央銀行に先行したのがオーストラリアだ。オーストラリア準備銀
行（RBA）は、中東情勢が緊迫化する以前の 2026 年 2 月に、政策金利を 3.85％へ引き上げた。
RBA は、民間需要が想定以上に拡大して需給がひっ迫し、インフレ率が当面目標を上回る可能
性が高いことを理由に挙げている 3。その後も利上げを重ね、足元では政策金利が 2008 年以来
の高水準に達している。 
これに対し、日本は利上げの時期がその他の国・地域と異なる。日本銀行は、2024 年 3 月の
マイナス金利政策解除以降、他国・地域が利下げを進める中でも段階的な利上げを続けてきた。
2026 年に入ってからも 6 月と 9 月に利上げを実施し、直近の政策金利は 1.25％となっている。

1 アメリカ、ユーロ圏、日本、イギリス、スイス、カナダ、オーストラリア、ニュージーランド、スウェーデ
ン、ノルウェーの 10 カ国・地域。 
2 スイスは同期間に会合を開催していないため、集計から除いている。 
3 Reserve Bank of Australia, "Statement by the Monetary Policy Board: Monetary Policy Decision," 
Media Release No. 2026-03, 3 February 2026.

3 / 10

図表1：主要先進国・地域の政策金利の推移

（注）アメリカは FF 金利誘導目標レンジの中央値、ユーロ圏は預金ファシリティ金利を掲載。 
（出所）BIS、各国中央銀行、Haver Analytics より大和総研作成

インフレ動向：インフレ率は足元で再加速する国・地域が目立つ 
主要先進国・地域のインフレ率の推移を示したものが図表 2 だ。2022 年から 2023 年にかけ
てのピーク時と比べると全般に低下したものの、2026 年に入って再び加速し、物価安定目標を
上回って推移する国・地域が目立つ。 
インフレ再加速の背景には、2026 年 2 月末以降の中東情勢の緊迫化に伴うエネルギー価格の
上昇がある。主要国・地域について見ると、ユーロ圏は 2026 年初めに消費者物価指数の前年比
（以下同様）が一時 2％4を下回ったものの、その後急速に上昇し、直近では 4.0％と主要先進
国・地域の中でも高い水準にある。アメリカも 2026 年に入って上昇基調が強まり、5 月に
4.2％まで上昇した後、直近でも 3.7％と目標である 2％を大きく上回っている。イギリスは
2025 年後半の 4％弱から 2026 年半ばには 2％台後半まで鈍化したものの、直近では 3.5％と再
び加速している。日本は 2026 年初めに 1.5％を下回る水準まで低下したが、その後は持ち直し、
直近では 2.1％と再び 2％を上回った。スイスも直近では 1.5％まで高まっている。 
その他の国・地域では、オーストラリアやニュージーランド、ノルウェーなどが、物価安定
目標及び目標レンジを上回って推移している。中でもオーストラリアは、エネルギー価格の上
昇に加え、国内需要の強さを背景に、直近では 4.3％と高い伸びとなっている。一方、スウェ
ーデンは 9 月に 1.5％と目標を下回った。中央銀行であるリクスバンクによると、一時的な財
政措置による押し下げの影響を除くと 3％に達していた 5。直近は 1.1％とさらに低下している。
原油価格の上昇といった供給ショックは幅広い国・地域のインフレ率に影響を与えているが、
物価情勢に関してはばらつきが見られ、各中央銀行の政策対応に差異を生む要因の一つとなっ

4 日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏・イギリスは 2％の物価安定目標を掲げており、スイスは 0％以上 2％未満の物価
上昇率を物価安定と位置付けている。

6 / 10

３ 注目トピック：企業の期待インフレ率について 
企業の期待インフレ率が利上げの持続性を左右する 
市場でも 2027 年の利上げのタイミングが定まらない中で、各中央銀行が 2027 年以降も利上
げを続けるかどうかは、エネルギー価格の上昇などで押し上げられたインフレ率が、目標へ向
けて自然に低下していくかにかかっている。その鍵を握るのが、中長期の期待インフレ率が物
価安定目標の近傍で安定している（いわゆる「アンカー」されている）か否かだ。 
2025 年のジャクソンホール会議で報告された Nakamura, Riblier and Steinsson （2025）
は、中長期の期待インフレ率がアンカーされていれば、中央銀行は供給ショックによる物価
上昇を「見過ごす（look through）」ことができる が、それが可能なのは、期待インフレ率が
強固にアンカーされ、インフレ抑制への信認が厚い中央銀行に限られると論じている 7。期待
インフレ率がアンカーされていない中で物価上昇を見過ごし、利上げが物価上昇に追いつか
なければ、実質金利の低下が需要を刺激してさらに物価を押し上げるスパイラルに陥りかね
ない。同論文が、ショックを見過ごせるか否かを見極める重要な先行指標として中長期の期
待インフレ率を位置づけるのはこのためだ。中長期の期待インフレ率が上振れすれば、中央
銀行は利上げを続けざるを得なくなる。 
同論文が主に参照するのは専門家の予想だが、本レポートでは企業の期待インフレ率に注目
する。実際に財・サービスの価格や賃金を設定するのは企業であり、価格改定の機会が限られ
る企業は、次の改定までの物価上昇を見越して現在の価格を設定するためだ。実証研究でも、
期待インフレ率が高まった企業ほど自社の販売価格を引き上げることが示されている 8。企業
の期待インフレ率は、企業の価格・賃金設定と金融政策をつなぐ重要な変数といえる。

インフレ高進期に上振れした期待インフレ率は低下するも、足元では高水準に 
実際に日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏・イギリスの企業の期待インフレ率を見ると、2022 年から
23 年のインフレ高進期に上振れした後、いずれも低下したものの下げ止まり、足元では再び上
昇する動きもみられる（図表4）。  
2026 年 7-9 月期の 1 年先の期待インフレ率は、日本が 2.6％、アメリカが 3.3％、ユーロ圏
が 4.0％、イギリスが 3.1％だ。中長期（5 年先、イギリスは 3 年先）の期待インフレ率は、日
本が 2.5％、アメリカが 3.3％（年次調査のため 2025 年時点）、ユーロ圏が 5.2％、イギリスが
2.8％となっている 9。

7 Nakamura, E., V. Riblier and J. Steinsson (2025) "Beyond the Taylor Rule," NBER Working Paper, 
No. 34200. 
8 Coibion, O., Y. Gorodnichenko and T. Ropele (2020) "Inflation Expectations and Firm Decisions: 
New Causal Evidence," Quarterly Journal of Economics, 135(1), pp.165-219. 
9 日本は日本銀行「短観」の企業の物価見通し、アメリカはクリーブランド連銀、イギリスはイングランド銀
行、ユーロ圏は欧州中央銀行による企業サーベイ。各サーベイは対象企業や質問形式、調査頻度が異なるた
め、水準の比較は幅を持って解釈する必要がある。

### Page 7

7 / 10

期待インフレ率がアンカーされているかを見るうえでは、水準だけでなく、実績のインフレ
率にどの程度反応するか（感応度）や、ショックの後に元の水準へ戻るかが重要だ。短期の期
待インフレ率は、インフレ高進期に実績に連動して大きく上振れした。その後は低下したもの
の、上振れ前のデータがある日本とアメリカでは、当時の水準までは戻り切っていない。調査
の開始がインフレ高進期以降のイギリスとユーロ圏でも、いったん低下した後に上昇している。 
中長期の期待インフレ率 10は短期ほど振れていないが、日本では、5 年先の期待インフレ率
が 2％台半ばへ切り上がったまま推移している。特に、2026 年 4-6 月期には 2.6％と調査開始
以来の最高を記録し、7-9 月期にも 2.5％と高水準を維持した。 
なお、図表 4 では、各国・地域の企業サーベイの水準差を考慮し、実績のインフレ率が 2％
程度の場合に対応する企業の期待インフレ率の水準の目安を網掛けで示している。足元の主要
国・地域の期待インフレ率は、いずれもこのレンジを上回っており、とりわけ日本では上振れ
幅が大きい。データ制約の観点からレンジ自体は幅をもって見る必要があるものの、企業の期
待インフレ率が従来よりも上方シフトしている可能性を示唆していると言えよう。

図表4：日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏・イギリスの企業の期待インフレ率と実績のインフレ率

（注）実線は 1 年先、破線は中長期（日本・アメリカ・ユーロ圏は 5 年先、イギリスは 3 年先）の企業の期待イ
ンフレ率（全規模全産業ベース）。灰色線は実績のインフレ率で、日本は消費税調整済み CPI 総合、アメリカと
イギリスは CPI 総合、ユーロ圏は HICP 総合の前年比。いずれも四半期で、イギリスは月次データの四半期平
均、アメリカの 5 年先は年次データを線形補間。網掛けは、実績のインフレ率が 2％程度のときの企業の期待
インフレ率の目安。①1 年先と中長期の期待インフレ率をそれぞれ実績インフレ率の過去 2 年平均に回帰して
求めた実績 2％時の推計値と、②実績の過去 2 年平均が 1.5％～2.5％だった時期の中長期の期待インフレ率の
平均について、最小値から最大値までの範囲を示している。なお、推計期間はデータの利用可能期間等に応じ
て異なる。ユーロ圏の白丸（2026 年 7-9 月期）は標本変更の影響があるため、対象から除外。イギリスは該当
期間がないため、実績の前年比が 1.5％～2.5％だった時期の 1 年先と 3 年先の期待インフレ率の平均を参考値
として示している。 
（出所）各国統計、Haver Analytics より大和総研作成

10 ユーロ圏でも、5 年先の期待インフレ率が 4-6 月期の 3.1％から 7-9 月期には 5.2％へ上昇したが、当該調査
では初回回答の企業が標本の過半を占めており、ECB は数値で回答する設問の結果は現時点では解釈が難しい
としている。ECB (2026) "Survey on the Access to Finance of Enterprises in the euro area – Third
